The issue came to a head when he nearly fainted during a training drill and the department physician documented his significantly low weight, prompting a mandatory evaluation; during the consultation, he acknowledged he had been purposefully keeping his intake very low to maintain his size for work.\n- Chief Complaint: “I’ve been really strict about cutting back on what I eat to stay as light and quick as possible for the job, and it’s gotten out of hand.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I’ll just have a protein shake before my shift and maybe some grilled chicken at night, and I keep telling my wife I already ate at the station so I can skip real meals and not feel like I’m weighing myself down for drills and calls.\n- S044 (Low_Weight): I know the doc says I’m way under where I should be, but when I see the number on the scale dropping below the other guys’ weights, it feels like proof I’m doing something right and makes me want to push it even further.\n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even think about having a burger at a cookout or grabbing a donut at the station, I get this tight feeling in my chest and picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and everybody seeing that I’ve let myself go.\n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): When I look in the mirror, all I can focus on is any spot that doesn’t look completely cut, and I feel like if I’m not the leanest guy on my crew then I’m failing at being the strong, dependable firefighter I’m supposed to be.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Lately I’ve been so drained and lightheaded on calls that my captain pulled me from a live drill, and at home my wife says I barely join family dinners or outings anymore because I’m always worried the food or timing will mess up my routine.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Then, compare this to the anxiety of having your routine disrupted, which makes you avoid family dinners and outings because you are terrified that messing up your food or timing will ruin your progress and make you lose control (functional_impairment)."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Because of this, the department doctor says I'm way underweight for my height. I've also been feeling incredibly drained and lightheaded, to the point where I nearly fainted during a live training drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your weight or body shape is normal, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others might say you're underweight?\n\nPatient: Even though the department doctor says I'm way underweight, I don't see it that way because when I look in the mirror, I only focus on any spot that doesn't look completely cut. I feel like I'm failing if I'm not the leanest guy on my crew, so seeing the scale drop below the other guys' weights actually feels like proof that I'm doing something right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any recurring thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals or behaviors related to food, exercise, or your body shape that you feel driven to do, even if they interfere with your daily life or firefighter duties?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel driven to follow really rigid routines, like constantly checking the scale and scrutinizing my body in the mirror to make sure I look cut. I even avoid family dinners because I'm terrified that the food or timing will disrupt my strict eating schedule. These habits have definitely interfered with my life and job, especially since getting lightheaded from barely eating got me pulled from a live drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these rigid eating and exercise habits are aimed more at achieving a certain body shape or weight, or are they driven by a fear of gaining weight or a need to perform these rituals to feel in control or safe?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I'm obsessed with my body shape because I feel like I have to look completely cut and be the leanest guy on my crew to be a dependable firefighter. At the same time, I have a deep fear of gaining weight—even thinking about eating normal food makes my chest tight because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of gaining weight and your need to be 'cut' compare in intensity to your feelings of anxiety or discomfort when you can't follow your strict eating and exercise routines?"

That matches the anxiety of having my routine disrupted, which makes me avoid family outings because I'm terrified that messing up my food timing will ruin my progress and make me lose control.

I feel like I'm failing if I'm not the leanest guy on my crew, so seeing the scale drop below the other guys' weights actually feels like proof that I'm doing something right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any recurring thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals or behaviors related to food, exercise, or your body shape that you feel driven to do, even if they interfere with your daily life or firefighter duties?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel driven to follow really rigid routines, like constantly checking the scale and scrutinizing my body in the mirror to make sure I look cut. I'm obsessed with my body shape because I feel like I have to look completely cut and be the leanest guy on my crew to be a dependable firefighter. At the same time, I have a deep fear of gaining weight—even thinking about eating normal food makes my chest tight because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of gaining weight and your need to be 'cut' compare in intensity to your feelings of anxiety or discomfort when you can't follow your strict eating and exercise routines?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly intense and completely tied together. The fear of gaining weight or not looking cut gives me a physical tightness in my chest because I'm terrified of letting my crew down. That matches the anxiety of having my routine disrupted, which makes me avoid family outings because I'm terrified that messing up my food timing will ruin my progress and make me lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your firefighter duties and the need to be physically fit for the job are the primary drivers of your strict eating and exercise habits, or are these behaviors more about achieving a personal body ideal regardless of your profession?\n\nPatient: It definitely started as a way to improve my speed and agility for the job, but now it's turned into a personal obsession where my entire identity is tied to being the leanest guy on the crew. If I look in the mirror and see any spot that isn't completely cut, I feel like I'm failing at being the strong, dependable firefighter I'm supposed to be."

I've also been feeling incredibly drained and lightheaded, to the point where I nearly fainted during a live training drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your weight or body shape is normal, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others might say you're underweight?\n\nPatient: Even though the department doctor says I'm way underweight, I don't see it that way because when I look in the mirror, I only focus on any spot that doesn't look completely cut. I feel like I'm failing if I'm not the leanest guy on my crew, so seeing the scale drop below the other guys' weights actually feels like proof that I'm doing something right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any recurring thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals or behaviors related to food, exercise, or your body shape that you feel driven to do, even if they interfere with your daily life or firefighter duties?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel driven to follow really rigid routines, like constantly checking the scale and scrutinizing my body in the mirror to make sure I look cut. I even avoid family dinners because I'm terrified that the food or timing will disrupt my strict eating schedule. These habits have definitely interfered with my life and job, especially since getting lightheaded from barely eating got me pulled from a live drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these rigid eating and exercise habits are aimed more at achieving a certain body shape or weight, or are they driven by a fear of gaining weight or a need to perform these rituals to feel in control or safe?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I'm obsessed with my body shape because I feel like I have to look completely cut and be the leanest guy on my crew to be a dependable firefighter. At the same time, I have a deep fear of gaining weight—even thinking about eating normal food makes my chest tight because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of gaining weight and your need to be 'cut' compare in intensity to your feelings of anxiety or discomfort when you can't follow your strict eating and exercise routines?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly intense and completely tied together. The fear of gaining weight or not looking cut gives me a physical tightness in my chest because I'm terrified of letting my crew down. That matches the anxiety of having my routine disrupted, which makes me avoid family outings because I'm terrified that messing up my food timing will ruin my progress and make me lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your firefighter duties and the need to be physically fit for the job are the primary drivers of your strict eating and exercise habits, or are these behaviors more about achieving a personal body ideal regardless of your profession?\n\nPatient: It definitely started as a way to improve my speed and agility for the job, but now it's turned into a personal obsession where my entire identity is tied to being the leanest guy on the crew. If I look in the mirror and see any spot that isn't completely cut, I feel like I'm failing at being the strong, dependable firefighter I'm supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: When you experience anxiety from not following your eating or exercise routine, is it more about the fear of gaining weight and losing control over your body shape, or is it about the discomfort of not completing a ritual that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely about the fear of gaining weight and losing my lean shape, not about the ritual itself. I get this tight feeling in my chest because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder during a call. That makes me feel like I’d be letting my crew down and completely failing as a firefighter."
  }
]

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. I'm a firefighter, and I've been really strict about cutting back on what I eat to stay as light and quick as possible for the job, but it's gotten out of hand. I was actually sent here for a mandatory evaluation after my department physician stepped in.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'gotten out of hand' with your eating habits, and have you experienced any significant weight loss or other physical symptoms as a result?\n\nPatient: Most days I'm just having a protein shake and some grilled chicken, often lying to my wife about eating at the station so I can skip real meals. Because of this, the department doctor says I'm way underweight for my height. I've also been feeling incredibly drained and lightheaded, to the point where I nearly fainted during a live training drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your weight or body shape is normal, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others might say you're underweight?\n\nPatient: Even though the department doctor says I'm way underweight, I don't see it that way because when I look in the mirror, I only focus on any spot that doesn't look completely cut. I feel like I'm failing if I'm not the leanest guy on my crew, so seeing the scale drop below the other guys' weights actually feels like proof that I'm doing something right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any recurring thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals or behaviors related to food, exercise, or your body shape that you feel driven to do, even if they interfere with your daily life or firefighter duties?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel driven to follow really rigid routines, like constantly checking the scale and scrutinizing my body in the mirror to make sure I look cut. I even avoid family dinners because I'm terrified that the food or timing will disrupt my strict eating schedule. These habits have definitely interfered with my life and job, especially since getting lightheaded from barely eating got me pulled from a live drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these rigid eating and exercise habits are aimed more at achieving a certain body shape or weight, or are they driven by a fear of gaining weight or a need to perform these rituals to feel in control or safe?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I'm obsessed with my body shape because I feel like I have to look completely cut and be the leanest guy on my crew to be a dependable firefighter. At the same time, I have a deep fear of gaining weight—even thinking about eating normal food makes my chest tight because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of gaining weight and your need to be 'cut' compare in intensity to your feelings of anxiety or discomfort when you can't follow your strict eating and exercise routines?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly intense and completely tied together. The fear of gaining weight or not looking cut gives me a physical tightness in my chest because I'm terrified of letting my crew down. That matches the anxiety of having my routine disrupted, which makes me avoid family outings because I'm terrified that messing up my food timing will ruin my progress and make me lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your firefighter duties and the need to be physically fit for the job are the primary drivers of your strict eating and exercise habits, or are these behaviors more about achieving a personal body ideal regardless of your profession?\n\nPatient: It definitely started as a way to improve my speed and agility for the job, but now it's turned into a personal obsession where my entire identity is tied to being the leanest guy on the crew. If I look in the mirror and see any spot that isn't completely cut, I feel like I'm failing at being the strong, dependable firefighter I'm supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: When you experience anxiety from not following your eating or exercise routine, is it more about the fear of gaining weight and losing control over your body shape, or is it about the discomfort of not completing a ritual that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely about the fear of gaining weight and losing my lean shape, not about the ritual itself. I get this tight feeling in my chest because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder during a call. That makes me feel like I’d be letting my crew down and completely failing as a firefighter.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety primarily driven by the fear of not meeting your firefighter physical expectations or by a general fear of gaining weight and its implications on your body shape, regardless of your job?\n\nPatient: Those two fears are completely intertwined for me, because my identity as a firefighter is entirely tied to my weight and how I look in the mirror. Gaining weight makes me feel like I’ll be slower climbing ladders, and if I'm not the leanest, most \"cut\" guy on the crew, I feel like I'm failing at being a dependable firefighter.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling anxious about your weight or body shape, do you find yourself performing specific, repetitive behaviors, like excessive exercise or checking your body in the mirror, in an attempt to reduce your anxiety, or is your anxiety more focused on the fear of gaining weight and its impact on your firefighter role?"
  }

Just thinking about eating high-calorie foods makes my chest tight because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and letting my crew down.

His identity as a strong, reliable firefighter became increasingly tied to staying lighter than his peers, and he began to avoid family meals and social barbecues, improvising excuses to hide how little he was actually eating even as his wife worried about how drawn and tired he looked off shift. The issue came to a head when he nearly fainted during a training drill and the department physician documented his significantly low weight, prompting a mandatory evaluation; during the consultation, he acknowledged he had been purposefully keeping his intake very low to maintain his size for work.\n- Chief Complaint: “I’ve been really strict about cutting back on what I eat to stay as light and quick as possible for the job, and it’s gotten out of hand.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I’ll just have a protein shake before my shift and maybe some grilled chicken at night, and I keep telling my wife I already ate at the station so I can skip real meals and not feel like I’m weighing myself down for drills and calls.\n- S044 (Low_Weight): I know the doc says I’m way under where I should be, but when I see the number on the scale dropping below the other guys’ weights, it feels like proof I’m doing something right and makes me want to push it even further.\n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even think about having a burger at a cookout or grabbing a donut at the station, I get this tight feeling in my chest and picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and everybody seeing that I’ve let myself go.\n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): When I look in the mirror, all I can focus on is any spot that doesn’t look completely cut, and I feel like if I’m not the leanest guy on my crew then I’m failing at being the strong, dependable firefighter I’m supposed to be.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Lately I’ve been so drained and lightheaded on calls that my captain pulled me from a live drill, and at home my wife says I barely join family dinners or outings anymore because I’m always worried the food or timing will mess up my routine.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. I'm a firefighter, and I've been really strict about cutting back on what I eat to stay as light and quick as possible for the job, but it's gotten out of hand. I was actually sent here for a mandatory evaluation after my department physician stepped in.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'gotten out of hand' with your eating habits, and have you experienced any significant weight loss or other physical symptoms as a result?\n\nPatient: Most days I'm just having a protein shake and some grilled chicken, often lying to my wife about eating at the station so I can skip real meals. Because of this, the department doctor says I'm way underweight for my height. I've also been feeling incredibly drained and lightheaded, to the point where I nearly fainted during a live training drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your weight or body shape is normal, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others might say you're underweight?\n\nPatient: Even though the department doctor says I'm way underweight, I don't see it that way because when I look in the mirror, I only focus on any spot that doesn't look completely cut. I feel like I'm failing if I'm not the leanest guy on my crew, so seeing the scale drop below the other guys' weights actually feels like proof that I'm doing something right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience any recurring thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals or behaviors related to food, exercise, or your body shape that you feel driven to do, even if they interfere with your daily life or firefighter duties?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel driven to follow really rigid routines, like constantly checking the scale and scrutinizing my body in the mirror to make sure I look cut. I even avoid family dinners because I'm terrified that the food or timing will disrupt my strict eating schedule. These habits have definitely interfered with my life and job, especially since getting lightheaded from barely eating got me pulled from a live drill.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these rigid eating and exercise habits are aimed more at achieving a certain body shape or weight, or are they driven by a fear of gaining weight or a need to perform these rituals to feel in control or safe?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I'm obsessed with my body shape because I feel like I have to look completely cut and be the leanest guy on my crew to be a dependable firefighter. At the same time, I have a deep fear of gaining weight—even thinking about eating normal food makes my chest tight because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of gaining weight and your need to be 'cut' compare in intensity to your feelings of anxiety or discomfort when you can't follow your strict eating and exercise routines?\n\nPatient: They are both incredibly intense and completely tied together. The fear of gaining weight or not looking cut gives me a physical tightness in my chest because I'm terrified of letting my crew down. That matches the anxiety of having my routine disrupted, which makes me avoid family outings because I'm terrified that messing up my food timing will ruin my progress and make me lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your firefighter duties and the need to be physically fit for the job are the primary drivers of your strict eating and exercise habits, or are these behaviors more about achieving a personal body ideal regardless of your profession?\n\nPatient: It definitely started as a way to improve my speed and agility for the job, but now it's turned into a personal obsession where my entire identity is tied to being the leanest guy on the crew. If I look in the mirror and see any spot that isn't completely cut, I feel like I'm failing at being the strong, dependable firefighter I'm supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: When you experience anxiety from not following your eating or exercise routine, is it more about the fear of gaining weight and losing control over your body shape, or is it about the discomfort of not completing a ritual that you feel compelled to do?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely about the fear of gaining weight and losing my lean shape, not about the ritual itself. I get this tight feeling in my chest because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder during a call. That makes me feel like I’d be letting my crew down and completely failing as a firefighter.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety primarily driven by the fear of not meeting your firefighter physical expectations or by a general fear of gaining weight and its implications on your body shape, regardless of your job?\n\nPatient: Those two fears are completely intertwined for me, because my identity as a firefighter is entirely tied to my weight and how I look in the mirror. Gaining weight makes me feel like I’ll be slower climbing ladders, and if I'm not the leanest, most \"cut\" guy on the crew, I feel like I'm failing at being a dependable firefighter.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling anxious about your weight or body shape, do you find yourself performing specific, repetitive behaviors, like excessive exercise or checking your body in the mirror, in an attempt to reduce your anxiety, or is your anxiety more focused on the fear of gaining weight and its impact on your firefighter role?\n\nPatient: I do check myself in the mirror all the time, focusing on any spot that doesn't look completely cut. However, my deepest anxiety is definitely the fear of gaining weight and how it would affect my job. Just thinking about eating high-calorie foods makes my chest tight because I picture the extra weight slowing me down on a ladder and letting my crew down.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your primary distress comes from the fear of being overweight or the compulsive need to check and control your body shape and weight, or is it a combination where one drives the other?"
  }

That fear also keeps me obsessively monitoring the scale to ensure I stay lighter than the other guys, which is how I prove to myself that I'm still a dependable firefighter.

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
